The conjugation in <a href="#eq:wedge-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wedge-pairing">[eq:wedge-pairing]</a> is essential for this conclusion over complex subspaces.

<a id="eq:kernel-vectors"></a>
For $x\in\mathcal X$, orthogonality of the two positive output ranges gives $$(\widehat x\otimes\widehat x)^*Z(\widehat x\otimes\widehat x)
 =\operatorname{tr}\bigl[R(P_{\widehat x})S(P_{\widehat x})\bigr]=0.$$ For a positive matrix, $v^*Zv=0$ implies $Zv=0$. Hence $$\widehat x\otimes\widehat x\in\ker Z\qquad(x\in\mathcal X).
 \label{eq:kernel-vectors}$$ For the input $y$ in the second hypothesis, $S(P_{\widehat y})=\bigwedge^2(L(P_y))$ is positive definite, and so is $R(P_{\widehat y})=K S(P_{\widehat y})^{\mathsf T}K^*$. The trace of their product is strictly positive. Equation <a href="#eq:choi-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:choi-pairing">[eq:choi-pairing]</a> at $a=b=P_{\widehat y}$ therefore proves $Z\ne0$.

*Excluding product vectors from the range.* It remains to exclude a product vector from the range. The following zero-set argument uses the product-exclusion principle of Bennett and coauthors <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[Bennett1999](#ref-Bennett1999), Lemma 1], in the nonorthogonal form given by Pittenger <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[Pittenger2003](#ref-Pittenger2003), Section IV, Lemma 3], and is related to Parthasarathy’s polynomial interpolation construction <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[Parthasarathy2004](#ref-Parthasarathy2004), Proposition 1.3]. Suppose that a nonzero product $u\otimes v$ belongs to $\operatorname{ran}Z$. Since $Z$ is Hermitian, its range is the orthogonal complement of its kernel. Equation <a href="#eq:kernel-vectors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-vectors">[eq:kernel-vectors]</a> implies $$p_u(x)p_v(x)=0\quad(x\in\mathcal X),\qquad
 p_u(x)=u^*\widehat x,\quad p_v(x)=v^*\widehat x.$$ For example, $$p_u(x)=\sum_i\overline{u_{ii}}x_i^2
       +\sqrt2\sum_{i<j}\overline{u_{ij}}x_ix_j.$$ This is a homogeneous quadratic in $x$; only its fixed coefficients are conjugated. Its distinct monomials make it nonzero when $u\ne0$. The same holds for $p_v$, since $v\ne0$. Each polynomial therefore vanishes on at most nine members of $\mathcal X$. Their zero sets cannot cover all twenty members, because $9+9<20$. This contradiction proves that $\operatorname{ran}Z$ contains no nonzero product vector.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:product-range" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:product-range">6</a>, the nonzero positive matrix $Z$ is nonseparable. Lemma <a href="#lem:choi-separable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:choi-separable">5</a> then shows that the composite is not entanglement breaking. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:certificate"></a>
### An integer pencil and its exact certificate

This section verifies every hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#prop:tensor-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tensor-criterion">19</a> for an explicit map $L:M_{4}(\mathbb C)\to M_{4}(\mathbb C)$. Four integer $6$-by-$4$ matrices $M_0,\ldots,M_3$ define $L$ and the pencil $M(x)=\sum_{i=0}^3x_iM_i$. The maximal minors of this pencil identify all singular projective directions. Exact reductions at three primes, together with the quotient and Galois arguments below, control quadratic evaluations on every ten of those directions.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The explicit matrix pencil

<a id="eq:explicit-matrices"></a>
<a id="eq:L"></a>
Use indices $0,1,2,3$ and define real $6$-by-$4$ matrices $$\begin{aligned}
M_0&=\begin{pmatrix}
6&0&0&0\\0&6&0&0\\0&0&6&0\\0&0&0&6\\0&0&0&0\\0&0&0&0
 \end{pmatrix},&
M_1&=\begin{pmatrix}
-12&0&0&0\\0&-6&0&0\\0&0&6&0\\0&0&0&12\\-6&0&6&6\\-6&-6&6&0
 \end{pmatrix},\\[2mm]
M_2&=\begin{pmatrix}
0&6&-2&0\\6&6&0&2\\-2&0&10&0\\0&2&0&24\\0&0&0&6\\0&-6&6&-6
 \end{pmatrix},&
M_3&=\begin{pmatrix}
0&0&-4&-3\\0&-2&-3&-4\\-4&-3&11&6\\-3&-4&6&25\\6&6&0&0\\0&-6&6&6
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:explicit-matrices}$$ For $x=(x_0,x_1,x_2,x_3)\in\mathbb C^4$, put $M(x)=\sum_{i=0}^3x_iM_i$ and define the complex-linear map $L$ on matrix units by $$L(E_{ij})=M_i^{\mathsf T}M_j\qquad(0\leq i,j<4).
 \label{eq:L}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-pencil-ppt"></a>
<div id="lem:pencil-ppt" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:gram-identity"></a>
**Lemma 20**. *The map $L:M_{4}(\mathbb C)\to M_{4}(\mathbb C)$ in <a href="#eq:L" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L">[eq:L]</a> is PPT. For every $x\in\mathbb C^4$, $$L(P_x)=M(x)^{\mathsf T}\overline{M(x)}=M(x)^*M(x),\qquad
 \operatorname{rank}L(P_x)=\operatorname{rank}M(x).
 \label{eq:gram-identity}$$ Moreover, $L(P_{e_0})=36I_4$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pencil-ppt"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pencil-ppt</summary>

<a id="eq:block-symmetry"></a>
*Proof.* Direct integer multiplication gives $$M_i^{\mathsf T}M_j=M_j^{\mathsf T}M_i\qquad(0\leq i,j<4).
 \label{eq:block-symmetry}$$ The exact checker in Appendix <a href="#app:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:arithmetic">11</a> verifies these equalities. For each row $r$, let $Q_r$ be the $4$-by-$4$ matrix whose $i$th column is the transpose of row $r$ of $M_i$. Comparing entries gives $L(A)=\sum_{r=1}^6Q_rAQ_r^*$, so $L$ is CP. Equation <a href="#eq:block-symmetry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-symmetry">[eq:block-symmetry]</a> also makes every $M_i^{\mathsf T}M_j$ symmetric. Therefore $T_4\circ L=L$, proving the PPT property.

For complex $x$, expansion of <a href="#eq:L" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L">[eq:L]</a> gives $$L(P_x)=\sum_{i,j}x_i\overline{x_j}M_i^{\mathsf T}M_j
       =M(x)^{\mathsf T}\overline{M(x)}.$$ Interchanging $i,j$ and using <a href="#eq:block-symmetry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-symmetry">[eq:block-symmetry]</a> gives the second expression in <a href="#eq:gram-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gram-identity">[eq:gram-identity]</a>. Since $v^*M(x)^*M(x)v=\left\lVert M(x)v\right\rVert^2$, the kernel of this Gram matrix is $\ker M(x)$, which proves the rank equality. Finally, $M_0^{\mathsf T}M_0=36I_4$. ◻

</details>

The positive-definite input required by the tensor criterion is now available. The remaining assertion concerns every complex rank-loss direction of the pencil.

<a id="stmt-prop-twenty-directions"></a>
<div id="prop:twenty-directions" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (Twenty rank-loss directions with independent quadratic evaluations). *The projective directions $[x]\in\mathbb P^3(\mathbb C)$ for which $\operatorname{rank}M(x)<4$ consist of exactly twenty distinct points. Each has a unique representative $x=(1,t_1,t_2,t_3)$. Every nonzero homogeneous quadratic in $\mathbb C[x_0,x_1,x_2,x_3]$ vanishes on at most nine of these representatives.*

</div>

The equation $M(x)y=0$ with $x,y\ne0$ is related to the bilinear kernel geometry studied by Hansen, Hauge, Myrheim, and Sollid for $4$-by-$4$ PPT states whose matrix and partial transpose both have rank six <a id="pm-cite-61"></a>[[HansenEtAl2012](#ref-HansenEtAl2012), Sections 2.2 and 7]. Their generic count concerns twenty product rays $x\otimes y$. The quadratic condition here concerns the vectors $x\otimes x$ for this specific pencil, and the following argument proves it from exact data.

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:twenty-directions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:twenty-directions">21</a>.* The maximal-minor relations first show that the affine quotient is nonzero and spanned by twenty monomials. We then construct a formal multiplication matrix. Its irreducible characteristic polynomial, certified modulo $41$, proves that the spanning monomials are independent and that the quotient is a degree-twenty field. This identifies all twenty complex directions. Finally, reductions at $131$ and $139$ give the full symmetric Galois action, which transports one independent set of ten quadratic evaluations to every such set. The three general algebraic facts used along the way are stated at their uses; their proofs are collected in Appendix <a href="#app:certificate-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate-algebra">11.2</a>.

The checker in Appendix <a href="#app:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:arithmetic">11</a> supplies the integer block identities and the finite modular data used in these stages. It does not enumerate the twenty complex directions or their ten-point determinants. The quotient, projective, and Galois deductions are proved below.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### The maximal minors and a nonzero quotient

Because $M(x)$ has four columns, the condition $\operatorname{rank}M(x)<4$ is equivalent to simultaneous vanishing of its fifteen maximal minors. For each four-element subset $\rho\subset\{1,\ldots,6\}$, taken in lexicographic order with its rows in increasing order, let $\Delta_\rho(x)$ be the determinant of those rows of $M(x)$. Put $p_\rho(t)=\Delta_\rho(1,t_1,t_2,t_3)$ and define $$I=(p_\rho)_\rho\subset\mathbb Q[t_1,t_2,t_3],\qquad
 \mathcal A=\mathbb Q[t_1,t_2,t_3]/I.$$ These equations describe the singular directions in the chart $x_0=1$. We have not yet proved that $I$ is proper, or that this chart contains all the singular directions.

<a id="eq:monomial-order"></a>
<a id="eq:DH"></a>
Order monomials first by increasing total degree $d$ and, within degree $d$, by the exponent triples $$(a,b,d-a-b),\qquad a=0,\ldots,d,\quad b=0,\ldots,d-a,
 \label{eq:monomial-order}$$ in the displayed nested order. Let $v$ be the column of the twenty monomials of degree at most three, and let $w$ be the column of the fifteen monomials of degree four. The coefficients of the minors define integer matrices $D,H$ by $$(p_\rho(t))_\rho=Dv(t)+Hw(t),\qquad
 D\in\mathbb Z^{15\times20},\quad H\in\mathbb Z^{15\times15}.
 \label{eq:DH}$$

The exact coefficient calculation in Appendix <a href="#app:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:arithmetic">11</a> gives $\det H\equiv40\pmod{41}$, so $H$ is invertible over $\mathbb Q$. Define $C=-H^{-1}D$. In the quotient $\mathcal A$, Equation <a href="#eq:DH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:DH">[eq:DH]</a> gives the relations $w=Cv$, expressing each degree-four monomial as a linear combination of the twenty lower monomials.

The relations $w=Cv$ must describe a nonzero quotient before they can be used to count its points. We first verify this and show that the low monomials span. The existence argument uses the following intersection fact for $\mathbb P^3\times\mathbb P^3$.

<a id="stmt-lem-six-bilinear"></a>
<div id="lem:six-bilinear" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Six bilinear equations). *Any six complex bilinear forms on $\mathbb C^4\times\mathbb C^4$ have a common zero $(x,y)$ with $x\ne0$ and $y\ne0$.*

</div>

The proof is given in Appendix <a href="#app:certificate-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate-algebra">11.2</a>.

Apply the lemma to the six rows of $M(x)y$. It gives $x,y\ne0$ with $M(x)y=0$, so all $\Delta_\rho(x)$ vanish.

The homogeneous degree-four part of $p_\rho(t)$ is $\Delta_\rho(0,t_1,t_2,t_3)$. Equation <a href="#eq:DH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:DH">[eq:DH]</a> therefore gives $$\bigl(\Delta_\rho(0,t)\bigr)_\rho=Hw(t).$$ If a common zero had $x_0=0$, invertibility of $H$ would imply $w(t)=0$. The entries $t_1^4,t_2^4,t_3^4$ occur in $w$, so $t=0$, contradicting $x\ne0$. Thus every projective common zero lies in the chart $x_0=1$. Rescaling the zero furnished by the lemma gives an affine common zero of the $p_\rho$, proving that $I$ is proper and $\mathcal A\ne0$. The same argument has excluded all points at infinity.

In $\mathcal A$, the relations $w=Cv$ show that the twenty low monomials span. In a monomial of total degree at least four, substitute for any degree-four factor. Each resulting term has strictly smaller total degree. Induction reduces every monomial to a linear combination of the low monomials; this proves spanning without assuming unique reductions.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### A formal multiplication operator

<a id="eq:N"></a>
Multiplication matrices are a standard way to encode finite polynomial systems; see Sottile <a id="pm-cite-62"></a>[[Sottile2001](#ref-Sottile2001), Section 2.3]. The quotient $\mathcal A$ is now known to be nonzero and spanned by the twenty low monomials, but their independence remains to be proved. Take a formal twenty-dimensional rational vector space with basis $e_\alpha$ indexed by exponent triples $|\alpha|\leq3$, in the order <a href="#eq:monomial-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monomial-order">[eq:monomial-order]</a>. Define an endomorphism $N$ of this formal space and its characteristic polynomial by $$Ne_\alpha=
 \begin{cases}
 e_{\alpha+(1,0,0)},&|\alpha|\leq2,\\[1mm]
 \displaystyle\sum_{|\beta|\leq3}
 C_{\alpha+(1,0,0),\beta}e_\beta,&|\alpha|=3,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad f(z)=\det(zI_{20}-N).
 \label{eq:N}$$ The row index of $C$ in the second line is the indicated degree-four monomial. Let $\pi:\mathbb Q^{20}\to\mathcal A$ send $e_\alpha$ to the class of $t^\alpha$, and let $\mu_1$ be multiplication by the class of $t_1$. The spanning result makes $\pi$ surjective. By the definition of $N$, <a id="eq:intertwining"></a>$$\pi N=\mu_1\pi.
 \label{eq:intertwining}$$ In particular, $\ker\pi$ is an $N$-invariant rational subspace. We will use irreducibility of $f$ to show that this kernel is zero, which will identify $N$ with multiplication by $t_1$.

For a prime $p$ with $\det H\not\equiv0\pmod p$, set $$\mathbb Z_{(p)}=\{a/b:a,b\in\mathbb Z,\ p\nmid b\}.$$ The identity $H^{-1}=\operatorname{adj}(H)/\det H$ shows that the entries of $C,N$ and the coefficients of the monic polynomial $f$ lie in $\mathbb Z_{(p)}$. In particular their denominators are units at $p$, and reduction gives $\overline f=\det(zI_{20}-\overline N)\in\mathbb F_p[z]$. This is a statement of integrality at the specified prime; no global integrality of the coefficients of $f$ is needed.

For $k\geq1$, put $$d_p(k)=\deg\gcd(\overline f,z^{p^k}-z).$$ The exact calculation in Appendix <a href="#app:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:arithmetic">11</a> gives the computed columns of Table <a href="#tab:modular-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:modular-certificate">1</a>. Its output also records all twenty-one characteristic coefficients and all twenty gcd degrees at each prime.

<div id="tab:modular-certificate">

|                |                 |                                                |          |
|:--------------:|:---------------:|:-----------------------------------------------|:---------|
|      $p$       | $\det H\bmod p$ |                                                |          |
|  gcd degrees   |                 |                                                |          |
| factor degrees |                 |                                                |          |
|       41       |       40        | $d_{41}(k)=0\ (1\leq k<20),\ d_{41}(20)=20$    | $20$     |
|      131       |       23        | $d_{131}(1)=1,\ d_{131}(2)=3,\ d_{131}(17)=18$ | $1,2,17$ |
|      139       |        9        | $d_{139}(1)=1,\ d_{139}(19)=20$                | $1,19$   |

Computed modular data and the factor degrees deduced from them.

</div>

The determinant residues justify all three local reductions. The primes exceed twenty, so the divisions in the Newton identities used to compute the characteristic polynomials are valid. Their primality follows by trial division by the primes at most eleven.

We explain the last column of the table. Over $\mathbb F_p$ the polynomial $z^{p^k}-z$ is squarefree, since its derivative is $-1$. Its irreducible factors are precisely the monic irreducible polynomials whose degrees divide $k$: a root of an irreducible polynomial of degree $s$ has a Frobenius orbit of length $s$. Hence $d_p(k)$ is the sum of the degrees of the *distinct* irreducible factors of $\overline f$ whose degrees divide $k$. At $p=41$, a factor of degree $s<20$ would make $d_{41}(s)$ positive, so $\overline f$ is irreducible of degree twenty. At $p=131$, the three displayed values force one factor of degree one, one of degree two, and one of degree seventeen. These degrees already sum to twenty, so there are no further or repeated factors. The two values at $p=139$ similarly force one factor of degree one and one of degree nineteen, without repetition. All three reductions are squarefree.

The following elementary reduction lemma lets us infer rational irreducibility without assuming that $f$ has integer coefficients. Its $p$-adic form is used in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:local-frobenius" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-frobenius">24</a> in Appendix <a href="#app:certificate-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate-algebra">11.2</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-monic-local-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:monic-local-reduction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Monic local reduction). *Let $p$ be a prime, and let the coefficient field and its valuation ring be one of the pairs $$(\mathbb L,R_p)=(\mathbb Q,\mathbb Z_{(p)})
 \quad\text{or}\quad
 (\mathbb L,R_p)=(\mathbb Q_p,\mathbb Z_p).$$ If a monic polynomial $P\in R_p[z]$ factors as $P=gh$ with monic $g,h\in\mathbb L[z]$, then $g,h\in R_p[z]$, and their reductions modulo $p$ retain their degrees, including degree zero. Consequently, if $P$ has positive degree and irreducible reduction in $\mathbb F_p[z]$, then $P$ is irreducible over $\mathbb L$.*

</div>

The proof is given in Appendix <a href="#app:certificate-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate-algebra">11.2</a>.

Applying the lemma to $f\in\mathbb Z_{(41)}[z]$ and its irreducible reduction proves that $f$ is irreducible over $\mathbb Q$.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The coordinate field and its twenty points

We can now prove independence of the twenty spanning monomials. An endomorphism with irreducible characteristic polynomial has no nonzero proper invariant subspace: a basis adapted to such a subspace makes the matrix block triangular and factors its characteristic polynomial into those of the restriction and quotient. Since $\pi$ is surjective and $\mathcal A\ne0$, its kernel is proper. Irreducibility of $f$ forces $\ker\pi=0$. The twenty low monomials are therefore a basis of $\mathcal A$, and $N$ is its multiplication matrix for $t_1$.

<a id="eq:coordinate-field"></a>
By Cayley–Hamilton the minimal polynomial of $N$ divides $f$. It is nonconstant, so irreducibility of $f$ makes it equal to $f$. In a nonzero unital algebra, a polynomial in an element vanishes exactly when the same polynomial in its multiplication operator vanishes; the reverse implication follows by applying the operator to $1$. Hence the subalgebra generated by $t_1$ is $\mathbb Q[z]/(f)$ and has dimension twenty. It is already all of $\mathcal A$: $$\mathcal A\simeq\mathbb Q[z]/(f),\qquad z\longmapsto t_1.
 \label{eq:coordinate-field}$$ In particular $\mathcal A$ is a field. Write the classes of $t_2,t_3$ as $h_2(t_1),h_3(t_1)$ with $h_2,h_3\in\mathbb Q[z]$ of degree below twenty.

<a id="eq:split-algebra"></a>
<a id="eq:points"></a>
The irreducible polynomial $f$ is separable in characteristic zero. Let its distinct complex roots be $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_{20}$. Extending scalars in <a href="#eq:coordinate-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coordinate-field">[eq:coordinate-field]</a> and applying the Chinese remainder theorem gives $$\mathbb C[t_1,t_2,t_3]/I\mathbb C[t_1,t_2,t_3]
 \simeq\mathbb C[z]/(f)\simeq\prod_{j=1}^{20}\mathbb C.
 \label{eq:split-algebra}$$ The factors are evaluations at the distinct triples $$t^{(j)}=(\lambda_j,h_2(\lambda_j),h_3(\lambda_j)).
 \label{eq:points}$$ Conversely, any complex common zero of the $p_\rho$ defines a unital $\mathbb C$-algebra homomorphism from the left side of <a href="#eq:split-algebra" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:split-algebra">[eq:split-algebra]</a> to $\mathbb C$, and such a homomorphism selects one factor of the product. Thus the triples in <a href="#eq:points" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:points">[eq:points]</a> are all the affine common zeros. The product of fields also shows that the complex ideal is radical, so these twenty points are reduced. Together with the exclusion of $x_0=0$, this identifies all complex projective singular directions of the pencil.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Local Frobenius and quadratic evaluations

It remains to prove that no nonzero quadratic vanishes on ten of the directions. We will show that the Galois group of the splitting field permutes the twenty roots arbitrarily. This transports independence for one set of ten evaluations to every set of ten.

The following is the locally integral form of the Frobenius factorization principle, often called Dedekind’s theorem. Milne states the monic integer case <a id="pm-cite-63"></a>[[Milne2022](#ref-Milne2022), Theorem 4.28]. We give the local rational argument needed for the coefficients of $f$.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-frobenius"></a>
<div id="lem:local-frobenius" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Locally integral Frobenius). *Let $F\in\mathbb Q[z]$ be monic, separable, and of positive degree, with coefficients in $\mathbb Z_{(p)}$. If its reduction in $\mathbb F_p[z]$ is squarefree with irreducible-factor degrees $s_1,\ldots,s_h$, then the Galois group of its rational splitting field contains an element with cycle lengths $s_1,\ldots,s_h$ on the roots.*

</div>

The proof is given in Appendix <a href="#app:certificate-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate-algebra">11.2</a>.

<a id="eq:galois-group"></a>
Let $\mathbb K\subset\mathbb C$ be a splitting field of $f$, and let $G=\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb K/\mathbb Q)$ act on its twenty roots. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:local-frobenius" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-frobenius">24</a> to the reductions at $131$ and $139$ gives elements of cycle types $(1,2,17)$ and $(1,19)$. Irreducibility of $f$ makes this action transitive. A nineteen-cycle fixes one root and is transitive on the other nineteen, so the stabilizer of that root is transitive on its complement. Transitivity conjugates this property to every point stabilizer; hence $G$ is transitive on ordered pairs of distinct roots, or two-transitive. The seventeenth power of an element of type $(1,2,17)$ is a transposition. Two-transitivity sends its two entries to any ordered pair, and conjugation therefore gives every transposition. Thus $$G=S_{20}.
 \label{eq:galois-group}$$ This is the elementary permutation criterion recorded in Milne <a id="pm-cite-64"></a>[[Milne2022](#ref-Milne2022), Lemma 4.31]. The group in <a href="#eq:galois-group" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:galois-group">[eq:galois-group]</a> is the splitting-field group acting on the twenty roots.

Form the $20$-by-$10$ evaluation matrix over $\mathbb K$ $$\mathcal E_{j,\alpha}=(t^{(j)})^\alpha,\qquad
 1\leq j\leq20,\quad |\alpha|\leq2.$$ Its entries lie in $\mathbb K$ by <a href="#eq:points" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:points">[eq:points]</a>. The ten column monomials belong to the basis of $\mathcal A$ already proved above. They remain independent after scalar extension to $\mathbb K$, where the split algebra is $\mathbb K^{20}$ through evaluation at the twenty triples. Hence $\mathcal E$ has column rank ten over $\mathbb K$, so some ten-row square submatrix has nonzero determinant.

Every $\sigma\in G$ permutes the triples in <a href="#eq:points" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:points">[eq:points]</a> according to its permutation of the roots, since $h_2,h_3$ have rational coefficients. In particular $\sigma(\mathcal E_{j,\alpha})=\mathcal E_{\sigma(j),\alpha}$. The group $S_{20}$ is transitive on ten-element subsets. Applying a suitable $\sigma$ to the known nonzero determinant therefore gives, up to a row-order sign, the determinant for any prescribed ten rows. A field automorphism preserves nonzero elements, so every such determinant is nonzero in $\mathbb K$ and hence in $\mathbb C$. The Galois action has been used only on algebraic determinants; their nonvanishing gives invertibility for arbitrary complex coefficient vectors.

Every homogeneous quadratic has a unique expression $$q(x_0,x_1,x_2,x_3)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq2}
 a_\alpha x_0^{2-|\alpha|}
 x_1^{\alpha_1}x_2^{\alpha_2}x_3^{\alpha_3}.$$ Its dehomogenization $q(1,t)$ is nonzero exactly when $q$ is nonzero. If it vanished at ten triples, the corresponding invertible evaluation matrix would force every $a_\alpha=0$. Thus it vanishes on at most nine triples. The identification of all common zeros above, the exclusion of $x_0=0$, and the equivalence between vanishing of the maximal minors and $\operatorname{rank}M(x)<4$ now prove Proposition <a href="#prop:twenty-directions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:twenty-directions">21</a>.0◻

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-frobenius"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-frobenius</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main-pair">2</a>.* Take $L$ from <a href="#eq:L" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L">[eq:L]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:pencil-ppt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pencil-ppt">20</a> supplies its PPT property, the rank identity, and the positive-definite input $L(P_{e_0})=36I_4$. Proposition <a href="#prop:twenty-directions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:twenty-directions">21</a> supplies the twenty distinct directions and the quadratic bound. Proposition <a href="#prop:tensor-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tensor-criterion">19</a> therefore applies, with the explicit maps in <a href="#eq:phi-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phi-pair">[eq:phi-pair]</a> determined by the integer matrices <a href="#eq:explicit-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:explicit-matrices">[eq:explicit-matrices]</a>.

The positive test also gives an exact nonzero Choi value. Since $\widehat{e_0}=e_{00}$, Equations <a href="#eq:SR" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:SR">[eq:SR]</a> and <a href="#eq:choi-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:choi-pairing">[eq:choi-pairing]</a> yield $$S(P_{e_{00}})=R(P_{e_{00}})=36^2I_6,\qquad
 (e_{00}\otimes e_{00})^*Z(e_{00}\otimes e_{00})
 =6\cdot36^4=10{,}077{,}696>0.$$ Thus the constructed pair has the asserted nonzero product-free composite Choi range, and its composite is not entanglement breaking. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:applications"></a>
### The state and a trace-preserving channel

We now combine the two independent inputs to the main state theorem: the operational obstruction for the represented class and the product-free Choi range of the explicit dimension-ten pair.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-frobenius-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-frobenius</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main-state" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main-state">1</a>.* Take $\Phi_1,\Phi_2$ from <a href="#eq:phi-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phi-pair">[eq:phi-pair]</a> and put $Z=J(\Phi_2\circ\Phi_1)$. Theorem <a href="#thm:main-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main-pair">2</a> proves that $Z$ is nonzero and that its range contains no nonzero product vector. It is positive because $\Phi_2\circ\Phi_1$ is CP. Thus $\lambda=\operatorname{tr}Z>0$, and $\rho=Z/\lambda$ is a density operator on $\mathbb C^{10}\otimes\mathbb C^{10}$ with the same range as $Z$. Lemma <a href="#lem:product-range" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:product-range">6</a> makes $\rho$ entangled.

Both maps are PPT. Lemma <a href="#lem:choi-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:choi-transfer">9</a>, with $a=b=c=10$, gives $$\rho=(\Phi_1^\sharp\otimes\Phi_2)
       \left(\frac{\Omega_{10}\Omega_{10}^*}{\lambda}\right)
       \in\mathscr C.$$ In particular, the representing vector is $\Omega_{10}/\sqrt{\lambda}$; normalization is by the actual Choi trace. Theorem <a href="#thm:zero-key" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:zero-key">17</a> now gives <a href="#eq:main-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-gap">[eq:main-gap]</a> for every $n\geq1$ and every protocol and ideal bit in its stated scope, including the purification and complete public record. The same theorem gives $K_D(\rho)=0$. All matrices defining $\rho$ are specified by <a href="#eq:explicit-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:explicit-matrices">[eq:explicit-matrices]</a> and <a href="#eq:phi-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phi-pair">[eq:phi-pair]</a>, so the state is explicit. ◻

</details>

The maps $\Phi_1,\Phi_2$ need not preserve trace. The next proposition converts any square PPT pair into a trace-preserving PPT channel, while retaining the pair’s composite Choi matrix in a local corner. The two-block switch follows Christandl, Müller-Hermes, and Wolf <a id="pm-cite-65"></a>[[CMHW2019](#ref-CMHW2019), Section IV.A]. The enlarged-input absorbing flag follows Filippov’s trace-preserving extension <a id="pm-cite-66"></a>[[Filippov2021](#ref-Filippov2021), Proposition 3, Equation (15)].

<a id="stmt-prop-channel-completion"></a>
<div id="prop:channel-completion" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:choi-corner"></a>
**Proposition 25**. *Let $\Psi_1,\Psi_2:M_{m}(\mathbb C)\to M_{m}(\mathbb C)$ be PPT maps. There are explicitly defined constants $c_1,c_2>0$, a trace-preserving PPT channel $\Theta:M_{2m+1}(\mathbb C)\to M_{2m+1}(\mathbb C)$, and a real coordinate isometry $W_0:\mathbb C^m\to\mathbb C^{2m+1}$ such that $$\label{eq:choi-corner}
 (W_0^*\otimes W_0^*)J(\Theta\circ\Theta)(W_0\otimes W_0)
       =c_1c_2J(\Psi_2\circ\Psi_1).$$ If $J(\Psi_2\circ\Psi_1)$ is nonseparable, then $\Theta\circ\Theta$ is not entanglement breaking.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-channel-completion"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-channel-completion</summary>

*Proof.* Define <a id="eq:channel-effects"></a>$$\label{eq:channel-effects}
 A_i=\Psi_i^\dagger(I_m),\qquad
 c_i=\frac{1}{1+\operatorname{tr}A_i},\qquad
 F_i=I_m-c_iA_i \quad (i=1,2).$$ The adjoint of a CP map is CP, so $A_i\succeq0$. Since $A_i\preceq(\operatorname{tr}A_i)I_m$, we have $F_i\succeq(1-c_i\operatorname{tr}A_i)I_m=c_iI_m\succ0$. The adjoint identity, using that $A_i$ is Hermitian, gives $$\operatorname{tr}\Psi_i(B)=\operatorname{tr}(A_iB),\qquad
 \operatorname{tr}B-c_i\operatorname{tr}\Psi_i(B)=\operatorname{tr}(F_iB)$$ for every complex matrix $B$. Hence $c_i\Psi_i$ is trace nonincreasing on positive inputs, and the positive effect $F_i$ records the missing trace.

<a id="eq:channel"></a>
Use the standard-coordinate decomposition $$\mathbb C^{2m+1}=\mathbb C^m_0\oplus\mathbb C^m_1\oplus\mathbb Ce_\star,$$ and let $W_0,W_1$ be the real coordinate inclusions. For $X\in M_{2m+1}(\mathbb C)$ write $X_{jj}=W_j^*XW_j$, $x_\star=e_\star^*Xe_\star$, and $P_\star=e_\star e_\star^*$. Set $$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:channel}
 \Theta(X)={}&c_2W_0\Psi_2(X_{11})W_0^*
             +c_1W_1\Psi_1(X_{00})W_1^* \notag\\
 &+\bigl(\operatorname{tr}(F_1X_{00})+\operatorname{tr}(F_2X_{11})+x_\star\bigr)P_\star .
\end{aligned}$$ The formula discards the off-diagonal input blocks. The two moving branches are CP compositions. For the flag branches, take spectral decompositions $F_i=\sum_r\lambda_{ir}v_{ir}v_{ir}^*$ and set $$K_{ir}=\sqrt{\lambda_{ir}}\,e_\star v_{ir}^*W_{i-1}^*
       \quad(i=1,2).$$ Then $\operatorname{tr}(F_iX_{i-1,i-1})P_\star=\sum_r K_{ir}XK_{ir}^*$. The remaining flag term is $P_\star XP_\star$. Thus $\Theta$ is CP. Since $c_iA_i+F_i=I_m$, for every complex $X$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{tr}\Theta(X)
 &=\operatorname{tr}\bigl[(c_1A_1+F_1)X_{00}\bigr]
   +\operatorname{tr}\bigl[(c_2A_2+F_2)X_{11}\bigr]+x_\star\\
 &=\operatorname{tr}X_{00}+\operatorname{tr}X_{11}+x_\star=\operatorname{tr}X.
\end{aligned}$$ The discarded blocks have zero trace, so $\Theta$ is trace preserving.

Because $W_0,W_1,e_\star$ have real coordinates, output transposition in <a href="#eq:channel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:channel">[eq:channel]</a> replaces $\Psi_i$ by $T_m\circ\Psi_i$ in the moving branches and fixes the flag output. Coordinate transpose is complex-linear: it does not conjugate the possibly complex scalar multiplying $P_\star$. The effects $F_i$ need not be real. Each transposed moving branch is CP by the PPT hypothesis, and the flag branches are CP as shown above. Therefore $T_{2m+1}\circ\Theta$ is CP, proving that $\Theta$ is PPT.

<a id="eq:square-corner"></a>
The flag is absorbing: $\Theta(P_\star)=P_\star$. Applying <a href="#eq:channel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:channel">[eq:channel]</a> twice to an input on the first block gives, for every $B\in M_{m}(\mathbb C)$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \Theta^2(W_0BW_0^*)
 ={}&c_1c_2W_0\Psi_2(\Psi_1(B))W_0^*\\
 &+\bigl(\operatorname{tr}(F_1B)+c_1\operatorname{tr}(F_2\Psi_1(B))\bigr)P_\star .
\end{aligned}$$ Compression to that block consequently gives $$\label{eq:square-corner}
 W_0^*\Theta^2(W_0BW_0^*)W_0
       =c_1c_2\Psi_2(\Psi_1(B)).$$ Expanding in the coordinate matrix units of the input turns <a href="#eq:square-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:square-corner">[eq:square-corner]</a> into <a href="#eq:choi-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:choi-corner">[eq:choi-corner]</a>. The coordinate qualification fixes the reference factor: for general complex isometries $W,V$ and a compatible map $\Gamma$, $$J\bigl(B\mapsto V^*\Gamma(WBW^*)V\bigr)
   =(W^{\mathsf T}\otimes V^*)J(\Gamma)(\overline W\otimes V).$$ Here $W_0$ is real, so the reference factor is exactly the one displayed in <a href="#eq:choi-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:choi-corner">[eq:choi-corner]</a>.

Local compression preserves separability, since it takes a positive product $A\otimes B$ to $(W_0^*AW_0)\otimes(W_0^*BW_0)$. Thus separability of $J(\Theta^2)$ would imply separability of the positive multiple $c_1c_2J(\Psi_2\circ\Psi_1)$ in <a href="#eq:choi-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:choi-corner">[eq:choi-corner]</a>. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-channel-completion-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-channel-completion</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main-channel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main-channel">3</a>.* Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:channel-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:channel-completion">25</a> with $m=10$ and $\Psi_i=\Phi_i$ from <a href="#eq:phi-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phi-pair">[eq:phi-pair]</a>. Their composite Choi matrix is nonseparable by Theorem <a href="#thm:main-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main-pair">2</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:product-range" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:product-range">6</a>. Formula <a href="#eq:channel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:channel">[eq:channel]</a> therefore gives the required PPT channel on $M_{21}(\mathbb C)$. ◻

</details>

The product-free range assertion is used for the compressed matrix $Z$. The full channel Choi matrix has a product contribution from the absorbing flag: since $\Theta$ annihilates off-diagonal blocks involving $e_\star$ and $\Theta(P_\star)=P_\star$, the matrix $P_\star\otimes P_\star$ is a direct summand of $J(\Theta^2)$. The channel conclusion is the nonseparability of the full Choi matrix, certified by its entangled corner.

<a id="sec:projection"></a>
### An alternative construction from projection overlaps

The dimension-ten construction is complete. Sections <a href="#sec:projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:projection">9</a> and <a href="#sec:combinatorial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:combinatorial">10</a> now produce another entangled member of $\mathscr C$ in much larger finite dimensions. The real construction has an additional symmetry: the same trace-preserving rectangular PPT channel acts on both halves of a maximally entangled state. These are parallel local actions on two subsystems, distinct from the self-composition of a square channel in Theorem <a href="#thm:main-channel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main-channel">3</a>.

The mechanism also differs from the dimension-ten range obstruction. The input consists of real unit vectors and projective measurements. The vectors produce a large sum of correlations, whereas zero trace overlaps among the projections restrict the supports of the product vectors in a hypothetical separable decomposition. These two bounds will conflict. Trace Gram matrices of positive factors also occur in completely positive semidefinite factorizations <a id="pm-cite-67"></a>[[PSVW2018](#ref-PSVW2018)]; here the zero pattern of such trace pairings supplies the support restriction.

<a id="eq:projection-overlap"></a>
For self-adjoint projections $E,F$, their trace overlap is always a nonnegative real number: $$\label{eq:projection-overlap}
 \operatorname{tr}(EF)=\operatorname{tr}(FEF)=\operatorname{tr}\bigl((EF)^*EF\bigr)\geq0.$$ In particular, it vanishes exactly when $EF=0$. This fact does not require $E$ and $F$ to commute.

<a id="stmt-prop-projection-criterion"></a>
<div id="prop:projection-criterion" class="proposition">

**Proposition 26** (Rectangular projection criterion). *Let $Q,K,D$ be positive integers, let $\mathcal Q$ be a set of $Q$ elements, and let $u_q\in\mathbb R^K$, $q\in\mathcal Q$, be unit vectors. Put $c_{qr}=u_q^{\mathsf T}u_r$. For each $q$, let $\{P_i:i\in\mathcal I_q\}$ be a finite projective measurement on $\mathbb C^D$: its members are self-adjoint projections, they are mutually orthogonal, and their sum is $I_D$. Zero projections are allowed. Take the label sets $\mathcal I_q$ disjoint, put $\mathcal I=\bigsqcup_{q\in\mathcal Q}\mathcal I_q$, and set $n=|\mathcal I|$. Suppose that*

1.  *$P_iP_j=P_jP_i$ whenever $i\in\mathcal I_q$, $j\in\mathcal I_r$, and $c_{qr}\ne0$;*

2.  *there is a real number $\kappa<Q/K$ such that every $J\subseteq\mathcal I$ satisfying $\operatorname{tr}(P_iP_j)>0$ for all distinct $i,j\in J$ has $|J|\leq\kappa$.*

<a id="eq:projection-map"></a>
*After ordering $\mathcal I$, define the complex-linear map $\Psi:M_{n}(\mathbb C)\to M_{D}(\mathbb C)$ on matrix units by $$\label{eq:projection-map}
 H_{ij}=c_{qr}P_iP_j,\qquad
 \Psi(E_{ij})=H_{ij}
 \quad(i\in\mathcal I_q,\ j\in\mathcal I_r).$$ Then $\Psi$ and its Hilbert–Schmidt adjoint $\Psi^\dagger:M_{D}(\mathbb C)\to M_{n}(\mathbb C)$ are PPT. Moreover, <a id="eq:projection-composite"></a>$$\label{eq:projection-composite}
 C:=J(\Psi^\dagger\circ\Psi)
 \quad\text{is nonseparable over }\mathbb C^n\otimes\mathbb C^n,
 \qquad \operatorname{tr}C=Q^2D.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-projection-criterion"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-projection-criterion</summary>

*Proof.* We first prove that $\Psi$ is PPT. To establish nonseparability, let $B$ be the compression of $C$ to the span of the vectors $e_i\otimes e_i$, expressed in that basis, and let $\mathbf1=(1,\ldots,1)^\mathsf T$. We will compare two bounds on the sum of its entries. The vector Gram matrix gives $$\mathbf1^*B\mathbf1\geq\frac QK\operatorname{tr}B,$$ whereas the zero tensor-basis diagonal entries would force a separable decomposition of $C$ to satisfy $\mathbf1^*B\mathbf1\leq\kappa\operatorname{tr}B$. We will also have $\operatorname{tr}B=QD>0$, so the strict inequality $\kappa<Q/K$ will make these bounds incompatible.

*The block Gram matrix and its partial transposes.* Regard each $u_q$ also as a vector in $\mathbb C^K$. For arbitrary $w_i\in\mathbb C^D$, self-adjointness of the projections gives <a id="eq:projection-block-gram"></a>$$\label{eq:projection-block-gram}
 \sum_{i,j\in\mathcal I}\langle w_i,H_{ij}w_j\rangle
 =
 \left\|
   \sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_q}u_q\otimes P_iw_i
 \right\|^2\geq0.$$ Thus the block matrix $H=[H_{ij}]_{i,j\in\mathcal I}=J(\Psi)$ is positive semidefinite. Lemma <a href="#lem:choi-separable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:choi-separable">5</a> implies that $\Psi$ is CP.

<a id="eq:projection-block-symmetry"></a>
Since $c_{qr}=c_{rq}$ is real, the first hypothesis gives $$\label{eq:projection-block-symmetry}
 H_{ij}=H_{ji}=H_{ij}^*.$$ Indeed, if $c_{qr}\ne0$, the two projections commute; if $c_{qr}=0$, both blocks vanish and no commutation is needed. Let $\Gamma_1=T_n\otimes\mathrm{id}_D$ and $\Gamma_2=\mathrm{id}_n\otimes T_D$ denote the partial transposes on the input and output Choi factors. Block symmetry gives <a id="eq:projection-partial-transposes"></a>$$\label{eq:projection-partial-transposes}
 H^{\Gamma_1}=H,\qquad
 H^{\Gamma_2}=H^{\mathsf T}\succeq0.$$ For the second identity, full transpose is the product of the two partial transposes. Full transpose preserves complex positive semidefiniteness: from $H=R^*R$ we get $H^{\mathsf T}=(\overline R)^*\overline R$. The Choi identities in Lemma <a href="#lem:choi-separable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:choi-separable">5</a> now show that $T_D\circ\Psi$ is CP. Hence $\Psi$ is PPT.

<a id="eq:projection-adjoint"></a>
<a id="eq:projection-adjoint-transpose"></a>
Lemma <a href="#lem:ppt-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ppt-stability">4</a> gives complete positivity and the PPT property for $\Psi^\dagger$. We will also use a more specific identity. Testing the defining adjoint relation on matrix units gives, for every $A\in M_{D}(\mathbb C)$, $$\label{eq:projection-adjoint}
 [\Psi^\dagger(A)]_{ij}=\operatorname{tr}(H_{ij}^*A).$$ Equation <a href="#eq:projection-block-symmetry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-block-symmetry">[eq:projection-block-symmetry]</a> therefore yields $$\label{eq:projection-adjoint-transpose}
 T_n\circ\Psi^\dagger=\Psi^\dagger$$ on all complex matrices, including non-Hermitian ones.

<a id="eq:projection-crossed-diagonal"></a>
<a id="eq:projection-compression"></a>
*The tensor-basis diagonal and a compression.* The composition $\Psi^\dagger\circ\Psi$ is CP, so $C\succeq0$. Index its rows and columns by $\mathcal I\times\mathcal I$. Equation <a href="#eq:projection-adjoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-adjoint">[eq:projection-adjoint]</a> gives the fully indexed formula <a id="eq:projection-composite-entries"></a>$$\label{eq:projection-composite-entries}
 C_{(i,a),(j,b)}=\operatorname{tr}(H_{ab}^*H_{ij}).$$ Since $c_{qq}=1$ and $P_i^2=P_i$, we have $H_{ii}=P_i$. The diagonal entries with indices $(i,j)$, and the entries of the compression $B$, are consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 C_{(i,j),(i,j)}&=\operatorname{tr}(P_iP_j),
       \label{eq:projection-crossed-diagonal}\\
 B_{ij}:=C_{(i,i),(j,j)}
   &=\operatorname{tr}(H_{ij}^*H_{ij})
     =c_{qr}^{\,2}\operatorname{tr}(P_iP_j)
       \quad(i\in\mathcal I_q,\ j\in\mathcal I_r).
       \label{eq:projection-compression}
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality follows from $\operatorname{tr}(P_jP_iP_iP_j)=\operatorname{tr}(P_iP_j)$, by idempotence and cyclicity of trace. It remains valid without commutation and when $c_{qr}=0$.

<a id="eq:projection-gram-bound"></a>
For each pair $q,r$, completeness of the two measurements gives $$\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_q}\sum_{j\in\mathcal I_r}\operatorname{tr}(P_iP_j)
 =\operatorname{tr}\!\left[
    \left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_q}P_i\right)
    \left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal I_r}P_j\right)
   \right]=D.$$ Summing <a href="#eq:projection-crossed-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-crossed-diagonal">[eq:projection-crossed-diagonal]</a> proves $\operatorname{tr}C=Q^2D$. The same identity and <a href="#eq:projection-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-compression">[eq:projection-compression]</a> give $$\label{eq:projection-gram-bound}
 \operatorname{tr}B=QD,\qquad
 \mathbf1^*B\mathbf1
   =D\sum_{q,r\in\mathcal Q}c_{qr}^{\,2}
   \geq\frac{DQ^2}{K}.$$ To see the inequality, set $M=\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}u_qu_q^{\mathsf T}$, a positive semidefinite $K$-by-$K$ real matrix. Then $\operatorname{tr}M=Q$ and $\operatorname{tr}(M^2)=\sum_{q,r}c_{qr}^2$. Cauchy–Schwarz applied to its $K$ nonnegative eigenvalues yields $\operatorname{tr}(M^2)\geq(\operatorname{tr}M)^2/K=Q^2/K$. Equivalently, the vector Gram matrix has rank at most $K$.

*The support bound forced by separability.* Suppose that $C$ is separable over $\mathbb C^n\otimes\mathbb C^n$. Spectrally decomposing its local positive factors and absorbing positive weights into the vectors gives a finite decomposition $$C=\sum_\ell
   (x^{(\ell)}\otimes y^{(\ell)})
   (x^{(\ell)}\otimes y^{(\ell)})^*,
 \qquad x^{(\ell)},y^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb C^n.$$ Whenever $\operatorname{tr}(P_iP_j)=0$, the corresponding tensor-basis diagonal entry is $$0=C_{(i,j),(i,j)}
   =\sum_\ell |x_i^{(\ell)}y_j^{(\ell)}|^2.$$ Each summand is nonnegative, so $x_i^{(\ell)}y_j^{(\ell)}=0$ for every $\ell$. For one summand put $z_i=x_i y_i$. If distinct $i,j$ lie in $\operatorname{supp}z$, then $x_i y_j\ne0$. The preceding implication and <a href="#eq:projection-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-overlap">[eq:projection-overlap]</a> force $\operatorname{tr}(P_iP_j)>0$. The second hypothesis therefore gives $|\operatorname{supp}z|\leq\kappa$.

Compressing the separable decomposition to the diagonal tensor subspace gives $B=\sum_\ell z^{(\ell)}(z^{(\ell)})^*$. Complex Cauchy–Schwarz on each support now yields <a id="eq:projection-separable-bound"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbf1^*B\mathbf1
  &=\sum_\ell\left|\sum_i z_i^{(\ell)}\right|^2
    \leq \kappa\sum_\ell\|z^{(\ell)}\|^2
     =\kappa\operatorname{tr}B\\
  &<\frac{Q}{K}\operatorname{tr}B
     =\frac{DQ^2}{K}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:projection-separable-bound}$$ The inequality is also valid for a zero $z^{(\ell)}$, whose support is empty. The strict step uses $\kappa<Q/K$ and $\operatorname{tr}B=QD>0$. This contradicts <a href="#eq:projection-gram-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-gram-bound">[eq:projection-gram-bound]</a> and proves nonseparability. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:projection-complex-transfer"></a>
The complex criterion has a state interpretation without the reality assumption. By <a href="#eq:projection-adjoint-transpose" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-adjoint-transpose">[eq:projection-adjoint-transpose]</a>, Lemma <a href="#lem:choi-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:choi-transfer">9</a> gives $$\label{eq:projection-complex-transfer}
 C=
 \bigl((\Psi^\dagger\circ T_D)\otimes\Psi^\dagger\bigr)
       (\Omega_D\Omega_D^*).$$ Both local maps in this formula satisfy the input-transpose CP conditions, as the transfer lemma asserts for the PPT pair $\Psi,\Psi^\dagger$. Thus $C/(Q^2D)\in\mathscr C$ also in the complex case, and Theorem <a href="#thm:zero-key" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:zero-key">17</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:operational-extensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:operational-extensions">18</a> apply to it. The two local maps in <a href="#eq:projection-complex-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-complex-transfer">[eq:projection-complex-transfer]</a> may differ; the reality assumption is what gives the same channel in <a href="#eq:projection-real-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-real-state">[eq:projection-real-state]</a>.

<a id="stmt-cor-real-channel"></a>
<div id="cor:real-channel" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:projection-real-state"></a>
**Corollary 27** (The same local channel for real projections). *Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-criterion">26</a>, suppose that every $P_i$ is real symmetric in the fixed basis of $\mathbb C^D$, and put $\Lambda=\Psi^\dagger:M_{D}(\mathbb C)\to M_{n}(\mathbb C)$. Then, on all complex inputs, <a id="eq:projection-real-invariances"></a>$$\label{eq:projection-real-invariances}
 \Lambda\circ T_D=\Lambda=T_n\circ\Lambda,
 \qquad \operatorname{tr}\Lambda(A)=Q\,\operatorname{tr}A.$$ In particular, $\Lambda/Q$ is a trace-preserving PPT channel. For $\Phi_D=\Omega_D/\sqrt D$, the normalized maximally entangled vector on $\mathbb C^D\otimes\mathbb C^D$, one has $$\label{eq:projection-real-state}
 \frac{C}{Q^2D}
 =
 \left(\frac{\Lambda}{Q}\otimes\frac{\Lambda}{Q}\right)
       (\Phi_D\Phi_D^*).$$ This is an entangled density operator on $\mathbb C^n\otimes\mathbb C^n$ in $\mathscr C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-real-channel"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-real-channel</summary>

*Proof.* Reality and <a href="#eq:projection-block-symmetry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-block-symmetry">[eq:projection-block-symmetry]</a> give the additional identity $H_{ij}^{\mathsf T}=H_{ij}$. Equation <a href="#eq:projection-adjoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-adjoint">[eq:projection-adjoint]</a> therefore becomes $$[\Lambda(A)]_{ij}=\operatorname{tr}(H_{ij}A),\qquad
 [\Lambda(T_D(A))]_{ij}=\operatorname{tr}(H_{ij}^{\mathsf T}A)=\operatorname{tr}(H_{ij}A).$$ This proves the first transpose invariance; the second is <a href="#eq:projection-adjoint-transpose" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-adjoint-transpose">[eq:projection-adjoint-transpose]</a>. Also, $$\operatorname{tr}\Lambda(A)=\sum_i\operatorname{tr}(H_{ii}A)
 =\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_q}\operatorname{tr}(P_iA)
 =Q\,\operatorname{tr}A.$$ Since $\Lambda$ is CP and PPT by the proposition, $\Lambda/Q$ is a trace-preserving PPT channel with input size $D$ and output size $n$.

<a id="eq:projection-real-identification"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:choi-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:choi-transfer">9</a> to the pair $\Psi,\Lambda$. Its first local factor is $$\Psi^\sharp=T_n\circ\Lambda\circ T_D=\Lambda$$ by the two invariances. Thus $$\label{eq:projection-real-identification}
 G:=(\Lambda\otimes\Lambda)(\Omega_D\Omega_D^*)=C.$$ Dividing this identity by $Q^2D$ gives <a href="#eq:projection-real-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-real-state">[eq:projection-real-state]</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-criterion">26</a> gives $\operatorname{tr}C=Q^2D>0$ and complex nonseparability. Finally, $\Lambda/Q$ and $(\Lambda/Q)\circ T_D$ are CP, so <a href="#eq:projection-real-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-real-state">[eq:projection-real-state]</a> is a representation of the form in Definition <a href="#def:represented-class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:represented-class">7</a>. This proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

The two channel uses in <a href="#eq:projection-real-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-real-state">[eq:projection-real-state]</a> act on separate subsystems. We next embed the rectangular maps into one matrix algebra to obtain a composition of square PPT maps.

<a id="stmt-cor-square-embedding"></a>
<div id="cor:square-embedding" class="corollary">

**Corollary 28** (Square embedding). *Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-criterion">26</a>, suppose in addition that $n\geq D$, and let $U:\mathbb C^D\to\mathbb C^n$ be the real coordinate isometry onto the first $D$ coordinates. Define <a id="eq:projection-square-pair"></a>$$\label{eq:projection-square-pair}
 \Xi_1=\operatorname{Ad}_U\circ\Psi,\qquad
 \Xi_2=\Psi^\dagger\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{U^*}
       =\Xi_1^\dagger .$$ Then $\Xi_1,\Xi_2:M_{n}(\mathbb C)\to M_{n}(\mathbb C)$ are PPT and $$\Xi_2\circ\Xi_1=\Psi^\dagger\circ\Psi.$$ Their composition is not entanglement breaking.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-square-embedding"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-square-embedding</summary>

*Proof.* Embedding and compression by $U$ are CP. The PPT composition stability in Lemma <a href="#lem:ppt-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ppt-stability">4</a> therefore applies to both maps. Directly, the reality of $U$ also gives $T_n(UAU^*)=U T_D(A)U^*$, while <a href="#eq:projection-adjoint-transpose" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-adjoint-transpose">[eq:projection-adjoint-transpose]</a> fixes the output transpose of the second map. The adjoint identity follows by reversing the two factors, and $U^*U=I_D$ gives the composite identity. Its Choi matrix is the nonseparable $C$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-criterion">26</a>; the characterization in Lemma <a href="#lem:choi-separable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:choi-separable">5</a> proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

The dimension comparison in this corollary is used only to choose the isometry $U$. The rectangular criterion itself has no such comparison. The displayed square maps need not preserve trace or be unital.

<a id="sec:combinatorial"></a>
### A finite combinatorial realization

We construct data satisfying Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-criterion">26</a>. A restricted-intersection bound gives real unit vectors such that every sufficiently large subfamily contains an orthogonal pair. The fiber argument for threefold tensor products strengthens this conclusion to eight mutually orthogonal members in every sufficiently large subfamily. Six of these eight vectors will index the six measurements in a parity obstruction; the remaining two receive a trivial measurement. Tensoring these measurements over the eight-subsets gives the projections required by the criterion. Because the projections are real, the resulting state is produced by the same trace-preserving PPT channel on both halves of a maximally entangled state.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Vectors with forced orthogonality

<a id="eq:combinatorial-inner-product"></a>
Set $p=257$, $k=4p=1028$, and $[k]=\{1,\ldots,k\}$. Define <a id="eq:combinatorial-base"></a>$$\label{eq:combinatorial-base}
 \begin{gathered}
 \mathcal U=\{A\subseteq[k]: |A|=2p,\ 1\in A\},\\
 b=|\mathcal U|=\binom{1027}{513}
              =\frac12\binom{4p}{2p},\\
 s_A=\frac{1}{\sqrt k}
       \bigl(2\mathbf1_{\{h\in A\}}-1\bigr)_{h=1}^k
       \in\mathbb R^k
       \quad(A\in\mathcal U).
 \end{gathered}$$ Each $s_A$ is a unit vector. Since $|A\mathbin{\triangle}B|=4p-2|A\cap B|$, counting agreeing and disagreeing coordinates gives $$\label{eq:combinatorial-inner-product}
 s_A^{\mathsf T}s_B
 =\frac{k-2|A\mathbin{\triangle}B|}{k}
 =\frac{|A\cap B|}{p}-1.$$ Thus two base vectors are orthogonal exactly when their sets intersect in $p$ elements. Put <a id="eq:combinatorial-delta"></a>$$\label{eq:combinatorial-delta}
 a_0=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\binom{k}{j}
     =\sum_{j=0}^{256}\binom{1028}{j},
 \qquad \delta=\frac{a_0}{b}.$$

Frankl and Rödl’s theory of forbidden intersections also forces orthogonal families of a prescribed size in large subsets of a sign cube <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[FR1987](#ref-FR1987), Theorem 1.11]. Here the precise bound follows from a direct polynomial argument and the fiber amplification below.

<a id="stmt-lem-restricted-intersections"></a>
<div id="lem:restricted-intersections" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (Restricted intersections). *If $\mathcal F\subseteq\mathcal U$ contains no two distinct sets whose associated vectors are orthogonal, then $|\mathcal F|\leq a_0=\delta b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-restricted-intersections"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-restricted-intersections</summary>

*Proof.* The restricted-intersection estimate belongs to the Frankl–Wilson theory <a id="pm-cite-69"></a>[[FW1981](#ref-FW1981)]. The proof below uses the multilinear-polynomial approach of Alon, Babai, and Suzuki <a id="pm-cite-70"></a>[[ABS1991](#ref-ABS1991)]. We give the needed specialization in full. The integer $257$ is prime: the primes at most $\sqrt{257}$ are $2,3,5,7,11,13$, and the respective remainders are $1,2,2,5,4,10$.

For distinct $A,B\in\mathcal F$, their common element $1$ and their equal sizes give $1\leq|A\cap B|\leq2p-1$. Equation <a href="#eq:combinatorial-inner-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-inner-product">[eq:combinatorial-inner-product]</a> excludes the value $p$. Hence $|A\cap B|$ is nonzero modulo $p$, whereas $|A\cap A|=2p$ is zero modulo $p$. Over the field $\mathbb F_p$, define $$f_A(x_1,\ldots,x_k)
 =1-\left(\sum_{h\in A}x_h\right)^{p-1}
 \quad(A\in\mathcal F).$$ For every nonzero $a\in\mathbb F_p$, multiplication by $a$ permutes the nonzero field elements; comparing their products gives $a^{p-1}=1$. Therefore, on the indicator vectors of the members of $\mathcal F$, $$f_A(\mathbf1_B)=
 \begin{cases}
  1,&A=B,\\
  0,&A\ne B.
 \end{cases}$$ Replace every positive power of each variable in every monomial by its first power. This preserves all evaluations on Boolean vectors and leaves degree at most $p-1$. The resulting multilinear polynomials remain linearly independent: evaluating a linear relation at each $\mathbf1_B$ makes every coefficient zero. The space of multilinear polynomials of degree at most $p-1$ has a basis consisting of the $a_0$ squarefree monomials of those degrees. Thus $|\mathcal F|\leq a_0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-fiber-amplification"></a>
<div id="lem:fiber-amplification" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (Fiber amplification). *For $t=1,2,3$, associate to $(A_1,\ldots,A_t)\in\mathcal U^t$ the unit vector $s_{A_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes s_{A_t}$. If $\mathcal R\subseteq\mathcal U^t$ contains no collection of $2^t$ distinct tuples whose associated vectors are pairwise orthogonal, then $$|\mathcal R|\leq t\delta b^t.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fiber-amplification"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fiber-amplification</summary>

*Proof.* The case $t=1$ is Lemma <a href="#lem:restricted-intersections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:restricted-intersections">29</a>. For $t=2$ or $3$, write $$\mathcal R_A
 =\{(A_2,\ldots,A_t):(A,A_2,\ldots,A_t)\in\mathcal R\},
 \qquad A\in\mathcal U.$$ Let $\mathcal G$ consist of those $A$ for which $\mathcal R_A$ contains $2^{t-1}$ tuples with pairwise orthogonal tensor vectors in the last $t-1$ factors. If distinct $A,B\in\mathcal G$ had $s_A\perp s_B$, choose one such collection in each fiber. Within either lifted collection, orthogonality comes from the last factors; between the two collections, it comes from the first factors. The two fibers are disjoint, so this would produce $2^t$ distinct tuples forbidden by the hypothesis. Thus $\mathcal G$ has no orthogonal base pair, and $|\mathcal G|\leq a_0=\delta b$ by the preceding lemma.

Each fiber in $\mathcal G$ has at most $b^{t-1}$ elements. Each other fiber has at most $(t-1)\delta b^{t-1}$ elements by the induction hypothesis. Summing these bounds and using at most $b$ fibers of the latter kind gives $$|\mathcal R|
 \leq (\delta b)b^{t-1}
      +b(t-1)\delta b^{t-1}
 =t\delta b^t.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:combinatorial-questions"></a>
<a id="eq:combinatorial-question-gram"></a>
<a id="eq:combinatorial-eight-bound"></a>
Use triples as measurement indices, called *questions*: $$\label{eq:combinatorial-questions}
 \begin{gathered}
 \mathcal Q=\mathcal U^3,\qquad Q=b^3,\qquad
 K=k^3=1028^3=1086373952,\\
 u_q=s_{A_1}\otimes s_{A_2}\otimes s_{A_3}\in\mathbb R^K
 \quad\bigl(q=(A_1,A_2,A_3)\bigr).
 \end{gathered}$$ The scalar target is $3\delta<1/K$: the support bound $3\delta Q$ must be strictly below $Q/K$ for the projection criterion. These vectors are unit vectors. If $q=(A_1,A_2,A_3)$ and $r=(B_1,B_2,B_3)$, their inner product is the rational number $$\label{eq:combinatorial-question-gram}
 c_{qr}=u_q^{\mathsf T}u_r
 =\prod_{h=1}^3\left(\frac{|A_h\cap B_h|}{257}-1\right).$$ An *orthogonal eight-set* will mean an eight-element subset of $\mathcal Q$ whose associated vectors are pairwise orthogonal. By Lemma <a href="#lem:fiber-amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fiber-amplification">30</a>, with $t=3$, every $\mathcal R\subseteq\mathcal Q$ containing no orthogonal eight-set satisfies $$\label{eq:combinatorial-eight-bound}
 |\mathcal R|\leq\kappa,\qquad
 \kappa:=3\delta Q=3a_0b^2.$$

<a id="eq:combinatorial-analytic-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:combinatorial-strict-bound"></a>
The next analytic estimate is what makes this bound strict enough for Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-criterion">26</a>. The central coefficient is largest among the $4p+1$ coefficients of $(1+1)^{4p}$, so $$b=\frac12\binom{4p}{2p}\geq\frac{16^p}{2(4p+1)}.$$ The binomial coefficients increase up to the central one, and the degree-$p$ term in the expansion of $(1+1/3)^{4p}$ is no greater than the full sum. Hence $$a_0\leq p\binom{4p}{p}
 \leq p(4/3)^{4p}3^p
 =p(256/27)^p
 <p\,12^p.$$ It follows that $$\label{eq:combinatorial-analytic-bound}
 \delta<2p(4p+1)(3/4)^p<2^{-65}.$$ For the last inequality, use $2p(4p+1)=528906<2^{20}$, $p=3\cdot85+2$, and $(3/4)^3=27/64<1/2$; the remaining factor $(3/4)^2$ is less than one. Also $1028<2^{11}$, so $$K<2^{33},\qquad
 3\delta<3\cdot2^{-65}<2^{-33}<\frac1K.$$ Combining this with <a href="#eq:combinatorial-eight-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-eight-bound">[eq:combinatorial-eight-bound]</a> proves $$\label{eq:combinatorial-strict-bound}
 \kappa=3a_0b^2<\frac QK.$$ The proof has now supplied the vector and cardinality ingredients. We next construct measurements that turn every orthogonal eight-set into a forbidden positive-overlap choice.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Six parity measurements

<a id="eq:combinatorial-context-signs"></a>
On $\mathbb C^2$, let $$X=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 Z=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 Y=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm i\\ \mathrm i&0\end{pmatrix}.$$ Use the Mermin–Peres square <a id="pm-cite-71"></a>[[Mermin1990](#ref-Mermin1990), [Peres1990](#ref-Peres1990), [Peres1991](#ref-Peres1991)]; its interpretation in the setting of quantum magic games is discussed by Arkhipov <a id="pm-cite-72"></a>[[Arkhipov2012](#ref-Arkhipov2012)]. The margins in the following array give the products of the three observables in each row and column: <a id="eq:combinatorial-parity-square"></a>$$\label{eq:combinatorial-parity-square}
 \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
 \begin{array}{ccc|c}
  X\otimes I_2&I_2\otimes X&X\otimes X&+I_4\\
  I_2\otimes Z&Z\otimes I_2&Z\otimes Z&+I_4\\
  X\otimes Z&Z\otimes X&Y\otimes Y&+I_4\\\hline
  +I_4&+I_4&-I_4&
 \end{array}$$ All nine entries are real symmetric involutions. In particular, if $J=\begin{psmallmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{psmallmatrix}$, then $Y=\mathrm iJ$ and $Y\otimes Y=-J\otimes J$, a real integral symmetric matrix. Order the six contexts as the three rows followed by the three columns, each read left to right or top to bottom. In context $t$, write the three observables as $(O_1,O_2,O_3)$. The first two commute and $$\label{eq:combinatorial-context-signs}
 O_3=e_tO_1O_2,\qquad
 (e_1,e_2,e_3,e_4,e_5,e_6)=(1,1,1,1,1,-1).$$ These identities follow from $XZ=-\mathrm iY$ and $ZX=\mathrm iY$. For example, the first two entries of the last row multiply, in either order, to $Y\otimes Y$; those of the last column multiply, in either order, to $-Y\otimes Y$. Since $O_3$ is a signed product of the first two, all three commute.

<a id="eq:combinatorial-context-projections"></a>
Let $\mathcal A=\{-1,+1\}^2$. The four outcomes of context $t$ are the matrices $$\label{eq:combinatorial-context-projections}
 h_t(\alpha,\beta)
 =\frac14(I_4+\alpha O_1)(I_4+\beta O_2),
 \qquad (\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal A.$$ The two factors divided by two are commuting spectral projections. Their products are therefore self-adjoint projections. Distinct outcomes are orthogonal, and the four products sum to $I_4$. They are real symmetric with rational entries, and their ranges have the three context eigensigns $(\alpha,\beta,e_t\alpha\beta)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-parity-obstruction"></a>
<div id="lem:parity-obstruction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Parity obstruction). *There is no choice of one projection from each of the six measurements in <a href="#eq:combinatorial-context-projections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-context-projections">[eq:combinatorial-context-projections]</a> such that the trace overlap of every two chosen projections from distinct contexts is strictly positive.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-parity-obstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-parity-obstruction</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose there were such a choice. All six selected projections would be nonzero. Let a selected row projection $E$ and column projection $F$ assign eigensigns $a,b\in\{-1,+1\}$ to their shared observable $O$. The eigenspace identities and self-adjointness give $$aEF=EOF=bEF.$$ If $a\ne b$, then $EF=0$ and their overlap is zero, contrary to the hypothesis. Thus the row and column assignments agree at each of the nine entries of the square.

The choice would consequently give nine consistent signs. The product of the three signs in context $t$ is $e_t$, by <a href="#eq:combinatorial-context-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-context-signs">[eq:combinatorial-context-signs]</a>. Multiplying the three row constraints gives product $+1$ for the nine signs; multiplying the three column constraints gives product $-1$. This is a contradiction. The argument applies to eigenspaces in the complex Hilbert space $\mathbb C^4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Tensor assembly and consequences

We now impose the six-context obstruction on every orthogonal eight-set of questions. Each such set receives its own tensor factor. Six of its questions use the six parity measurements on that factor, while all other questions use the trivial measurement with one outcome $I_4$ and three zero outcomes. Thus any positive-overlap choice containing the eight-set violates parity on its factor. This arrangement also ensures the commutation needed in the projection criterion: two distinct questions with nonzero vector inner product cannot both have nontrivial measurements on any one factor.

For a uniform index set, include a factor carrying only trivial measurements for every nonorthogonal eight-subset as well. Let <a id="eq:combinatorial-tensor-space"></a>$$\label{eq:combinatorial-tensor-space}
 \begin{gathered}
 \mathcal S=\{S\subseteq\mathcal Q:|S|=8\},\qquad
 L=|\mathcal S|=\binom Q8,\\
 \mathcal H=\bigotimes_{S\in\mathcal S}\mathbb C^4\cong\mathbb C^D,\qquad
 D=4^L.
 \end{gathered}$$ To fix all bases, order subsets of $[k]$ by their increasing lists, then order tuples lexicographically, and order the eight-subsets by their increasing lists in that order. Use the corresponding order for the tensor factors, the standard tensor-product basis in each $\mathbb C^4=\mathbb C^2\otimes\mathbb C^2$, and the lexicographic order on sign pairs with $-1<+1$.

For $S\in\mathcal S$ and $q\in\mathcal Q$, define a four-outcome measurement $h_{S,q}$ on the factor indexed by $S$. If $S$ is an orthogonal eight-set and $q$ is its $t$-th element with $1\leq t\leq6$, set $h_{S,q}=h_t$. In every other case, set <a id="eq:combinatorial-trivial-measurement"></a>$$\label{eq:combinatorial-trivial-measurement}
 h_{S,q}(+1,+1)=I_4,\qquad
 h_{S,q}(\alpha,\beta)=0
 \quad\text{when }(\alpha,\beta)\ne(+1,+1).$$ Thus the last two questions in an orthogonal eight-set use the trivial measurement, as do all questions outside that set and all questions on a nonorthogonal factor. Every decision of whether a factor is orthogonal is determined by the rational numbers in <a href="#eq:combinatorial-question-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-question-gram">[eq:combinatorial-question-gram]</a>.

<a id="eq:combinatorial-global-projections"></a>
For each $q$, retain an outcome string on every factor and define $$\label{eq:combinatorial-global-projections}
 \mathcal I_q=\{q\}\times\mathcal A^{\mathcal S},\qquad
 P_{q,\lambda}
   =\bigotimes_{S\in\mathcal S}h_{S,q}(\lambda_S)
   \quad\bigl(\lambda\in\mathcal A^{\mathcal S}\bigr).$$ Order these outcome strings lexicographically using the stated factor and sign-pair orders, and order the full labels first by question and then by outcome string. This is the standard basis order used for the concrete maps. The matrices $P_{q,\lambda}$ are real symmetric projections. For a fixed $q$, two distinct strings differ in some factor, where their local projections have zero product; hence their global projections are orthogonal. Summing over all strings factors as $\bigotimes_S\sum_{\alpha,\beta}h_{S,q}(\alpha,\beta)=I_D$. Keeping every outcome string, including those whose projection is zero, gives a uniform label set: there are exactly $4^L=D$ labels for every question. Hence <a id="eq:combinatorial-label-count"></a>$$\label{eq:combinatorial-label-count}
 m:=\left|\bigsqcup_{q\in\mathcal Q}\mathcal I_q\right|=QD.$$ In particular $m\geq D$, as required for the square embedding in Corollary <a href="#cor:square-embedding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:square-embedding">28</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-combinatorial-measurements"></a>
<div id="lem:combinatorial-measurements" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32**. *The vectors <a href="#eq:combinatorial-questions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-questions">[eq:combinatorial-questions]</a> and measurements <a href="#eq:combinatorial-global-projections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-global-projections">[eq:combinatorial-global-projections]</a> satisfy the two hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-criterion">26</a> with $n=m=QD$ and $\kappa=3a_0b^2<Q/K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-combinatorial-measurements"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-combinatorial-measurements</summary>

*Proof.* If $q=r$, projections with those labels belong to one projective measurement and commute. Suppose that $q\ne r$ and $c_{qr}\ne0$. They cannot both receive nontrivial contexts on any one factor: that would place them together in an orthogonal eight-set, forcing $c_{qr}=0$. On each factor at least one of the two selected local projections is consequently $0$ or $I_4$. They commute on each factor, so their tensor products commute. This proves the first hypothesis.

<a id="eq:combinatorial-overlap-product"></a>
Now let $J$ be a label set with strictly positive trace overlap between every distinct pair. Distinct labels for one question have zero product, so $J$ contains at most one label per question. The trace overlap of any two global projections factors as $$\label{eq:combinatorial-overlap-product}
 \operatorname{tr}(P_{q,\lambda}P_{r,\mu})
 =
 \prod_{S'\in\mathcal S}
 \operatorname{tr}\bigl(h_{S',q}(\lambda_{S'})
          h_{S',r}(\mu_{S'})\bigr).$$ Every local factor is nonnegative by <a href="#eq:projection-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-overlap">[eq:projection-overlap]</a>, whether or not the two local projections commute. Thus strict positivity of this finite product implies strict positivity of each factor.

If the questions represented by $J$ contained an orthogonal eight-set $S$, take the selected labels for its first six questions. At the factor indexed by $S$, equation <a href="#eq:combinatorial-overlap-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-overlap-product">[eq:combinatorial-overlap-product]</a> would give strictly positive overlap for each pair of the six selected context projections. Lemma <a href="#lem:parity-obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:parity-obstruction">31</a> forbids this. The represented questions therefore contain no orthogonal eight-set, and <a href="#eq:combinatorial-eight-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-eight-bound">[eq:combinatorial-eight-bound]</a> gives $|J|\leq\kappa$. For completeness, the pairwise condition on a singleton is vacuous even when its projection is zero; it still satisfies the bound because $a_0,b\geq1$ and $\kappa=3a_0b^2\geq3$. The strict inequality $\kappa<Q/K$ is <a href="#eq:combinatorial-strict-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-strict-bound">[eq:combinatorial-strict-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-thm-old-construction"></a>
<div id="thm:old-construction" class="theorem">

**Theorem 33** (Finite combinatorial construction). *Set <a id="eq:combinatorial-exact-data"></a>$$\label{eq:combinatorial-exact-data}
 \begin{gathered}
 p=257,\qquad k=1028,\qquad
 b=\binom{1027}{513},\qquad
 a_0=\sum_{j=0}^{256}\binom{1028}{j},\\
 Q=b^3,\qquad K=1086373952,\qquad
 \kappa=3a_0b^2<\frac QK,\\
 L=\binom Q8,\qquad D=4^L,\qquad m=QD.
 \end{gathered}$$ Use the vectors in <a href="#eq:combinatorial-questions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-questions">[eq:combinatorial-questions]</a>, the $D$ labels per question and projections in <a href="#eq:combinatorial-global-projections" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-global-projections">[eq:combinatorial-global-projections]</a>, and the map $\Psi:M_{m}(\mathbb C)\to M_{D}(\mathbb C)$ in <a href="#eq:projection-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-map">[eq:projection-map]</a>. Put $\Lambda=\Psi^\dagger$ and $\Phi_D=\Omega_D/\sqrt D$.*

<a id="eq:combinatorial-state"></a>
1.  *The map $\Lambda/Q:M_{D}(\mathbb C)\to M_{m}(\mathbb C)$ is a trace-preserving PPT channel, and $$\label{eq:combinatorial-state}
            \rho_{\mathrm{comb}}
            =\left(\frac{\Lambda}{Q}\otimes\frac{\Lambda}{Q}\right)
              (\Phi_D\Phi_D^*)
            =\frac{J(\Psi^\dagger\circ\Psi)}{Q^2D}$$ is an entangled density operator on $\mathbb C^m\otimes\mathbb C^m$ belonging to $\mathscr C$. It has $K_D(\rho_{\mathrm{comb}})=0$ and satisfies the uniform unhalved $1/5$ gap and all operational consequences of Theorem <a href="#thm:zero-key" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:zero-key">17</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:operational-extensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:operational-extensions">18</a>, with their stated protocol, purification, and completion hypotheses.*

<a id="eq:combinatorial-pair"></a>
2.  *For the real coordinate isometry $U:\mathbb C^D\to\mathbb C^m$ onto the first $D$ coordinates, the maps $$\Phi_1^{\mathrm{comb}}=\operatorname{Ad}_U\circ\Psi,\qquad
            \Phi_2^{\mathrm{comb}}=\Lambda\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{U^*}
                     =(\Phi_1^{\mathrm{comb}})^\dagger$$ are PPT maps on $M_{m}(\mathbb C)$, and $$\label{eq:combinatorial-pair}
            J(\Phi_2^{\mathrm{comb}}\circ\Phi_1^{\mathrm{comb}})
              =Q^2D\,\rho_{\mathrm{comb}}$$ is nonseparable over $\mathbb C^m\otimes\mathbb C^m$. In particular their composition is not entanglement breaking.*

<a id="eq:combinatorial-channel-corner"></a>
3.  *Let $\Theta_{\mathrm{comb}}$ be the channel obtained by applying Proposition <a href="#prop:channel-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:channel-completion">25</a> to this square pair. It is a trace-preserving PPT channel on $M_{2m+1}(\mathbb C)$. With the positive constants $c_1,c_2$ and the first coordinate inclusion $W_0$ from that proposition, the Choi matrix of its square has the corner $$\label{eq:combinatorial-channel-corner}
            (W_0^*\otimes W_0^*)J(\Theta_{\mathrm{comb}}^2)
            (W_0\otimes W_0)
            =c_1c_2Q^2D\,\rho_{\mathrm{comb}}.$$ Consequently $\Theta_{\mathrm{comb}}^2
             =\Theta_{\mathrm{comb}}\circ\Theta_{\mathrm{comb}}$ is not entanglement breaking.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-old-construction"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-old-construction</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:combinatorial-measurements" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:combinatorial-measurements">32</a> verifies the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-criterion">26</a>. Its composite $C=J(\Psi^\dagger\circ\Psi)$ is positive and nonseparable over $\mathbb C^m\otimes\mathbb C^m$, with $\operatorname{tr}C=Q^2D$. The projections are real symmetric, so Corollary <a href="#cor:real-channel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:real-channel">27</a> proves <a href="#eq:combinatorial-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-state">[eq:combinatorial-state]</a>, the channel assertion, and membership in $\mathscr C$. Theorem <a href="#thm:zero-key" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:zero-key">17</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:operational-extensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:operational-extensions">18</a> give the stated operational conclusions.

Since $m=QD\geq D$, Corollary <a href="#cor:square-embedding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:square-embedding">28</a> applies to the displayed coordinate isometry and gives the square PPT pair. Its composite is $\Psi^\dagger\circ\Psi$, and the real identification <a href="#eq:projection-real-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-real-identification">[eq:projection-real-identification]</a> gives <a href="#eq:combinatorial-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-pair">[eq:combinatorial-pair]</a>. Applying Proposition <a href="#prop:channel-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:channel-completion">25</a> to this pair gives the channel on $M_{2m+1}(\mathbb C)$ and its exact corner <a href="#eq:combinatorial-channel-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-channel-corner">[eq:combinatorial-channel-corner]</a>. The corner is nonseparable, so that proposition also proves that the channel square is not entanglement breaking. ◻

</details>

All these objects are given by finite exact formulas. The normalized sign vectors $s_A$ have coordinates $\pm1/\sqrt{1028}=\pm1/(2\sqrt{257})$, which are irrational because $257$ is prime. The Gram entries of the question vectors in <a href="#eq:combinatorial-question-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combinatorial-question-gram">[eq:combinatorial-question-gram]</a>, rather than the vector coordinates, enter the Choi blocks. Those Gram entries are rational; all nine local observables are integral, and all local and global projections are rational. Consequently every block $H_{ij}$, every coefficient of $\Psi,\Lambda,\Phi_1^{\mathrm{comb}},\Phi_2^{\mathrm{comb}}$, and every entry of $\rho_{\mathrm{comb}}$ is rational in the specified bases. The completion also has rational coefficients: its positive scalings and flag matrices in Proposition <a href="#prop:channel-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:channel-completion">25</a> are formed from the rational square maps using traces and rational operations.
